Thermal Dawn — Thermal Storage Is Cheap Storage

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Another great idea from Down Under: Thermal Dawn promises cost effective heating and cooling, mainly by replacing gas boilers in cold climates. CleanTechnica had a chance to interview Nick Zeniou, Founder of Thermal Dawn, recently by phone, fresh after his presentation at Everything Electric. You can hear him explain his revolutionary system here, or here with a little less drama.

Everything Electric was a good opportunity to measure public reaction to the new product. Nick tells me that hundreds of people checked out their stall. “People care about carbon cost and comfort. People don’t compromise on comfort.” Sounds like comfort trumps carbon cost. Homeowners who visited the stand and filled out the survey were focused on sustainability and cost. Nick cautions: “it was an electrification expo crowd, and 50 of the 62 already had solar, so it’s not the whole market.” See the chart below.

Although I live in the warmer part of Australia (Brisbane), many Aussies have to endure cold winters. Nick tells me he is looking at installs in Sydney, Canberra, and Melbourne in the deep south. Thermal Dawn can store heat up to 70 degrees Celsius, or down to 5 degrees. Thermal Dawns’ equipment can run cold or hot water through pipes and radiators that are already installed, depending on the need — without having to run an expensive gas boiler. He describes it as a thermal battery. Just to be clear, it does not generate electricity.

Check out this graph below, which shows the Thermal Dawn system in action at a house in Melbourne during a day in August 2026. It shows room temperature and when the heat pump was running, in 15-minute samples, unedited. The heat pump ran until about 1:45pm, then not again, heated by stored energy through the evening peak.

“What about return on investment,” I asked. He described two systems they have had running over the southern Australian winter which have saved the homeowners approximately AU$1,500 each. “The first was a house in Melbourne, our first install, live since May 2026. The owners charge the system in their retailer’s free three-hour midday window, so the heat for the evening costs them close to nothing. The second was a Warragul (slab-heated home, Victoria): installed in two days in July 2026. Our controls turn the home’s concrete slab into part of the battery, managing when it charges and when it releases heat. On 95% of winter evenings, the heat pumps did not run at all between 5 pm and 9pm. Since commissioning, 70,521 MJ of gas has been avoided by just these two installations.”

Nick further explains: “A standard heat pump draws from the grid during those expensive morning and evening hours. You’ve escaped gas, but you’re still paying premium rates every time the temperature drops at night. Thermal Dawn is different. Our system charges when power is cheapest, a free midday window, your own solar, or late-night off-peak, then delivers that stored warmth through your radiators and underfloor heating all evening, with the compressor off through the expensive hours.

It appears that Thermal Dawn’s secret sauce is “Phase Change Materials.” Naturally, Nick declined to share exactly what Thermal Dawn is using. The system replaces a gas boiler with a combination of reverse cycle heat pump, well insulated water tank, and phase change materials. PCMs are designed to freeze or melt at a specific temperature, storing and releasing heat when they change physical state, usually from solid to liquid. “PCM doubles our heat capacity.” The heating applications I could understand, what about the cooling? “Cool water from the tank runs through a fan coil unit, and the fan blows cool air into the room. Cooling is an optional add-on.” That’s the answer.

How do thermal storage and a home battery/solar system work together? “Thermal storage is cheap storage doing one job very well. Heating, cooling, and hot water are the largest, most predictable loads in the house, and covering them thermally is far cheaper per kWh than covering them from lithium. A battery is expensive storage doing a general-purpose job.

“The practical effect is that your battery stops being the thing that carries your heating. It’s freed up for lights, appliances, the EV, and overnight backup, and it lasts longer for not being cycled hard every winter evening. Homes with a battery already are often the clearest case, because the expensive storage stops being spent on the cheapest possible job.”

Thermal dawn connects to existing hydronic pipework. The gas boiler with its high gas bills is gone. Check out the animation of heat discharge overnight. “The thermal battery charged on solar during the day and now heats the house while the heat pump stays off. It’s an illustration of what the app shows, not a recording of a live home.” For those rooms that don’t have hydronic radiators or underfloor slab heating, Thermal Dawn installs fan coil units. Nick tells me via a followup email that: “It’s a small indoor unit with a water coil and a fan: warm water from the thermal battery runs through the coil, and the fan blows the heating or cooling into the room.”

The heavy technical discussion out of the way, I asked Nick about his background. He grew up in an isolated part of New Zealand. His job was to collect the firewood for the family’s heat source — a fireplace. He had to get the fire going by 4:00 pm or the house would not warm up for the cold evening. Timing was very important, as it is for electricity use.

Nick is a mechanical engineer who has worked across tech companies and startups. While working on installing commercial air conditioning, he came across the idea that became Thermal Dawn. Nick joined the dots between the huge variations in electricity supply cost throughout each 24-hour period, with an average home’s thermal energy needs (up to two-thirds of consumption) and the fact that homes are becoming power stations. Thence to thermal batteries powered by Thermal Dawn technology.

He reminisces about the start of the business: “When our first unit was finished, we loaded it into a ute at 5pm and I drove it down the Hume overnight, Sydney to Melbourne (1000 km), to install it the next morning. We had a four-month-old at home at the time. My wife Linda is my co-founder and leads mechanical design and manufacturing, so while I was on the road, she was looking after a newborn.”

For those who would like to get involved, Nick invites Australian homeowners to sign up here and they’ll be in touch. “Readers overseas: send us a message or request a quote at the same link to register interest. If we hear from enough people in one area, we can bring forward our plans to come there.”

Having proven their point, Thermal Dawn is cleared to sell and installing its first batch now, with a larger product for ducted air conditioning in development. I asked about apartment block heating and Nick confirmed that they are looking at it but want to get the domestic home sorted first.

I asked a couple of followup questions “What about the Victorian government’s position?” Nick’s answer:  “Victoria is set to bring in support for systems like ours from the middle of next year. With the state election in November, there’s talk of a change in direction and of belt-tightening, so we’ve built Thermal Dawn to stand on its own. It saves households money without a subsidy, and any support is a bonus.”

Any plans for installs in Canberra? “Yes. Canberra is one of the best places in Australia for this: cold winters, plenty of hydronic homes, and a territory already moving off gas. We’re talking with installers there now.”

I really appreciate these technological experts when they take the time to explain things to me. Sometimes I feel like Winnie the Pooh — a bear of very little brain. Looks like Thermal Dawn has solved another part of the energy puzzle — good news for homeowners and also for the planet.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

Unpatched Bitcoin Lightning nodes face channel-draining bug

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Core Lightning urges Bitcoin node operators to patch payment and channel security flaws and tighten remote-access credentials.

Core Lightning, software for operating Bitcoin Lightning payment nodes, is urging users to install v26.06.10 to address channel-security, payment-resolution, and remote-access flaws.

Maintainers also recommend reviewing the credentials used for remote commands, making access restrictions a separate task alongside the software upgrade.

The release notice recommends upgrading as soon as practical. Maintainers have temporarily withheld security-fix tests to slow the conversion of patches into working exploits and give operators more time to upgrade.

The new version adds checks on a peer's transaction signatures during Bitcoin dual-funded channel opening and splicing, following the Lightning protocol's BOLT 2 specification, according to the tagged changelog.

A conditional Bitcoin payment contract, known as an HTLC, that had just been removed could be confused with a live one sharing its payment hash, amount and expiry. The node could then fail the live payment upstream even though its peer could still claim it.

Another fix rejects channel opens when a peer proposes a dust limit above 5,000 satoshis. The changelog says the previous behavior let a peer trim the node's balance into fees during a forced close. Dual-funded opens also reject a peer's dust limit when it exceeds the channel reserve.

Dual funding remains experimental, and maintainers discourage zero-confirmation channels with untrusted peers. The release also refuses a v2 zero-confirmation open unless the operator has configured acceptance for that peer.

Nodes that have run master cannot downgrade into the 26.06.x series because their database schema is newer.

Version 26.06.10 adds software checks. Maintainers also advise operators to bind and review issued commando credentials.

Naples: an Italian-American investor ready to make an offer to De Laurentiis?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A new investor could soon knock on Aurelio De Laurentiis’ door. Matt Rizzetta, director of the investment fund Underdog Global Partners and owner of Napoli Basketball, has great ambitions for Neapolitan sport. In an interview given to TMWthe Italian-American businessman explained that he wanted to build a true multi-sport organization under a single brand. “We own Napoli Basketball and our vision is to create a formidable multi-sport organization, propelling Naples to the top of world sport, under one brand”he explained. From this perspective, Napoli Calcio would represent a major part of his project, even if the club currently remains the property of De Laurentiis.

Already the owner of the Campobasso club in Serie C (and in the past Ascoli), Rizzetta nevertheless assures that he is ready to take action if the Neapolitan president considered a sale. “If he wanted to sell on fair terms for both parties, we would be ready”he said, specifying that the operation would be mainly financed by several large American sports funds, with significant personal participation on his part. The investor even puts forward significant figures to give weight to his ambitions. “At present, our group and our financiers, mainly large American sports funds, have around 1 to 1.3 billion euros available”he revealed. Rizzetta indicates that it has already informed De Laurentiis of its availability, while recognizing that the latter has no obligation to sell. It remains to be seen whether the Napoli boss will be willing to open the door to a change of ownership.

Pamela Abdy on Life After Warner Bros: ‘Right Now, I’m Taking a Break and Chilling’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A month after her departure from Warner Bros. was announced, Pamela Abdy made her first industry appearance Thursday night at the Women in Film Honors at the Beverly Hilton.

On the morning of Oct. 1, Abdy was announced as WIF’s inaugural Vanguard Award recipient. Then, she was serving as co-chair and CEO of Warner Bros. Motion Picture Group with her longtime producing partner Michael DeLuca. Later that night, Abdy and DeLuca learned that would not usher the film studio into its next chapter amid the Skydance merger. Just over a month later, she was ready to talk about it. Related Stories Guillermo del Toro Tears Up as He Says ‘We’re Handing Down a World in Ruins’: ‘It Pains Me Deeply to See Three Generations Fail the Next’ Neon Partners With 'NAZA' Filmmakers, Guardian and MK2 to Bring Prize-Winning Doc to Wider Audiences

“Right now, I’m taking a break and chilling,” Abdy told Variety‘s Marc Malkin on the red carpet.

“I got to go see every single one of my daughter’s volleyball games, without being interrupted. So that’s what I’m doing right now,” she said.

When Malkin asked her about the swirling rumors about her next stop, including the idea that she and De Luca could fill the open film head job at Netflix, Abdy said, “When I decide what I’m doing, I promise I will tell everyone. But honestly, it’s an interesting time to be able to take a break, and really decide what I want.

“I know what happiness is when I’m working with filmmakers, and wherever and whatever I end up doing, that’s my goal is to continue to work with the best storytellers out there,” Abdy said.

When Malkin asked if she thinks the new combined company can reach its goal of releasing 30 movies a year, Abdy replied, “I don’t know. I’m not there. I hope they do.”

A month after her departure from Warner Bros. was announced, Pamela Abdy made her first industry appearance Thursday night at the Women in Film Honors, where she described what she's focusing on. “Right now, I’m taking a break and chilling,” she told Variety on the red carpet.… pic.twitter.com/p4YOj31oHv

The WIF prize recognizes the breadth of Abdy’s career beyond Warner Bros., where she oversaw blockbusters and award darlings including “One Battle After Another,” “Sinners,” “Barbie,” “Weapons,” and “Dune: Part Two.” Last year, Warner Bros. tied the all-time studio record with 11 Academy Awards from an industry-leading 30 nominations. That said, not all of the duo’s big-budget swings paid off. For example, “The Bride!” lost $100 million theatrically after failing to spark at the box office this spring. Then, “Digger,” Warner Bros.’ final release as a standalone studio, proved to be one of the biggest financial misfires of the year.

IREN’s bitcoin mining revenue drops 58% as firm plans full exit by year-end

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

IREN has been shifting its focus to the AI infrastructure business, and its AI cloud revenue rose 182% in the past quarter to $200 million.

IREN (IREN) reported bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) mining revenue of $28 million in the three months ended Sept. 30, which is a 58% quarter-over-quarter decline from $67 million and an 88% drop from $233 million a year earlier.

According to the company's 10-Q filing, IREN mined 402 BTC during the period, compared with 2,039 BTC in the same quarter last year. Average operating hashrate fell to 8.8 EH/s from 45.3 EH/s year-over-year. IREN held no bitcoin on its balance sheet as of the end of September as it liquidates mined bitcoin daily.

"We continue to operate Bitcoin miners at certain of our data centers while we transition this data center capacity toward AI Cloud," IREN wrote in the filing. "We aim to substantially complete this transition by December 31, 2026, with immaterial Bitcoin mining operations expected to continue through calendar year 2027."

IREN, which launched in 2018, announced in March last year that it will stop expanding its bitcoin mining operations and shift its focus to AI and high-performance computing through AI cloud services and data center businesses. Competing bitcoin miner firms MARA and CleanSpark have also turned to the AI infrastructure business.

Consequently, the company's quarterly revenue rose 66% sequentially to $228 million, driven by AI cloud revenue of around $200 million, which is up 182% from the prior quarter. The company posted a net loss of $263 million, narrower than the $684 million loss in the previous quarter, while adjusted EBITDA rose to $66 million from $19 million.

IREN said it has $20.6 billion in total assets, including $6 billion of data center assets, with assets and revenue underpinned by customer contracts with Nvidia, Microsoft, and 13 other customers.

The firm's Nasdaq-listed shares fell 17% on Thursday to close at $18.03, in line with a broader sell-off across AI and tech stocks.

This Home Locker Made My Entryway So Much More Functional

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mustard Made home lockers blend utilitarian functionality and bold color for the perfect entryway and mudroom furniture.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

I’m always on the hunt for products that nail the sweet spot between form and function. And with a toddler at home, let’s just say we’ve accumulated stuff. Toys, shoes, kid gear, you name it. Our entryway, laundry room and pretty much every shared space could use a little organizational intervention.

So, when I first saw Mustard Made lockers online, I was intrigued. The bold colors and playful, schoolhouse-inspired design, coupled with the range of sizes, made them feel like a storage upgrade with personality. I was looking for a compact catch-all near the front door for the shoes, hats and everyday essentials we’re constantly grabbing on our way out.

The more I thought about it, the more I liked the idea of a home locker: sturdy metal, magnetic doors and an easy-to-clean finish. Sold. I ordered the two-door Midi locker from Mustard.

Of course, choosing a color was a whole ordeal. I played it safe with Chalk, expecting a crisp white, but it turned out to be more of a warm off-white. It’s not quite what I pictured, but it works in our space. That said, if I could do it all over again, I’d tell myself to be bold and go with Mustard or Olive.

Here’s how the locker came together, how it’s holding up and whether it’s helping tame the everyday chaos.

A utilitarian storage cabinet that pairs versatile functionality with a pop of color

The Midi arrived on my porch in a flat, narrow box that was easy to lift and carry where I needed to build it. Once I unpacked everything, I was happy to find that most of the locker simply slots and clicks into place. No tools, screws or complicated assembly required. The only tool I needed was a Phillips-head screwdriver to attach the door handles.

Mustard Made includes printed instructions, and you can also find them on the website, along with a step-by-step assembly video if you need a little extra guidance.

The trickiest part? Getting the doors on. They can take a bit of fiddling, but gently bending the small tab helps hold the L-shaped bracket in place and keeps the door steady while you assemble it.

Once assembled, the locker feels solid, sturdy and made to last. But it’s the brand’s attention to detail that really makes it functional. There are attachment points on the back for securing it to the wall (a welcome feature with a toddler around), interior hooks on each door for hanging smaller items, adjustable shelves and even a cable hole in the back for managing cords.

And the customization options are a bonus, even though they’re sold separately. Mustard Made sells extra shelves, super-strong button magnets and metal hangers for the Skinny and Twinny lockers, which can double as mini wardrobes. They also offer adorable alphabet and number magnets for displaying messages, labeling spaces or giving older kids something to play with.

Another thing I love? Mustard Made offers six different locker styles and sizes, so there’s an option for just about every room and organizational storage need.

My daughter loves using the locker as a little art gallery, proudly displaying her masterpieces. And it’s not just for artwork. The home locker is a fun spot for handwritten notes, photos, collectible magnets and other little things we want to put on display. The Mustard Made button magnets are surprisingly strong and sturdy, too, holding much more than your average fridge magnet.

If you have things you want to keep under lock and key, Mustard Made has you covered. Each locker door comes with its own lock and set of keys. It took me a little while to realize that the locks are different, so a heads-up: each door comes with two keys, and you’ll want to keep them organized. Mixing them up (or losing them altogether) could lead to frustration.

In our house, the locker is primarily a shoe drop zone by the front door, so we haven’t had much reason to use the locks. But I can see this feature coming in handy for storing valuables or keeping potentially hazardous household items out of little hands. Just be sure to use appropriate child-safety measures for anything that poses a risk.

With so many shapes, sizes and colors to choose from, Mustard Made lockers are a versatile storage solution for just about any room. Think mudrooms, laundry rooms, playrooms, entryways, porches and beyond.

They’re not limited to hiding clutter, either. Depending on the style and size, they can pull double duty as entertainment consoles, wardrobes, buffet tables or even drink cabinets. Basically, they’re as flexible as they are functional.

A colorful home locker for your entryway, mudroom, playroom, garage, porch and beyond

You can shop the Midi and the full range of lockers directly from Mustard Made. The Midi retails for $369 and comes in 12 colors. Want something a little more design-forward? The brand also offers lockers with fluted glass doors for a more elevated look.

AI agents are a ‘bane’ for some website operators, says Cloudflare

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

CEO says sovereigns probably exploited PHP2SHELL ages ago, and suggests containers are dead

Nation-state actors and criminals likely exploited the PHP2SHELL vulnerability before its disclosure, according to Cloudflare CEO Matthew Prince.

“These vulnerabilities, like the PHP vulnerabilities that were disclosed the other day, had been in the wild for years,” Prince said on the company’s third quarter earnings call. “We're going back through our logs looking for any evidence that hackers or nation-state actors or others had been using them in the past. I know that we're going to find examples of that.”

That was just one of the warnings he offered on the call, among them that some of Cloudflare’s customers believe AI agents are “a bane to their business and want to keep them away.”

To explain why, Prince hypothesized that he could use an agent to find him a restaurant to eat at after he finishes his day of investor relations duties.

“It might go and scour the internet and look at 1,000 different restaurants' websites, pull down 1,000 different menus, and then I might choose to only go to one of those,” he said. “The problem is that 999 of those restaurants have now had a cost that is just been applied to them, but they are not getting any benefit.”

The CEO admitted that web searches have always driven some low-value traffic but feels the volume of agentic activity – which Cloudflare has previously predicted will be one thousand times greater than clicks from humans – means the problem is now at a different scale.

Prince also had bad news for Kubernetes aficionados, because he thinks containers might become another casualty of the agentic age.

The CEO allowed that containers are “a significant improvement over bare metal or even virtual machines,” and said they fueled the growth of hyperscale clouds and mobile applications. But he thinks containers are already cooked.

“The container-based architecture of the hyperscalers and neoclouds just can't keep up in a cost-effective way with the new agentic paradigms of today,” he argued, again citing the 1000:1 ratio of bot:human traffic Cloudflare expects to see online any year now.

That’s a Cloudflare-centric view, because Prince pitched the company’s serverless “Workers” service as an alternative to containers. The company also offers products that can either block agents, ameliorate the cost and traffic impacts of agentic activity, or allow organizations to monetize traffic generated by AI.

Prince said demand for those products is growing fast, claiming that since July 1st, “approximately five percent” of the web opted in to Cloudflare’s content protection products. The CEO said Meta is already a customer, and said Zuck’s new “Muse” agents will benefit Cloudflare by creating more agentic traffic that third parties want to tame.

He also pointed to year-on-year revenue growth of 39 percent, which saw quarterly revenue reach a record $782.5 million.

And he thinks there are better days ahead, due to the terrible state of security.

“It may seem like the sky is falling with the number of new security vulnerabilities that have been reported over the last few weeks,” Prince said. “I wish I could tell you we were through the worst, but we're not.”

He thinks Cloudflare can profit from security misery because the extent of its activities and client base mean it is “among the first to receive quiet disclosure of new vulnerabilities” and remediates them before disclosure.

Investors thought this all made sense, and in after-hours trading sent Cloudflare shares up by around 15 percent, meaning it recovered and then improved on losses that it incurred earlier after major US indices dropped around four percent as investors who worry tech stocks are overvalued. ®

The Urgency of Early ADHD Intervention

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Delaying treatment for ADHD until the teen years comes with potential life-long consequences. Here’s what you should know.

Q: I believe my elementary school-age child has ADHD, but my husband doesn’t think so. He refuses to get our child evaluated. I have ADHD and I know this can run in families. What should I do?

This question reflects a dilemma faced by many families: What happens when one parent wants possible signs of ADHD evaluated while another prefers to wait and see? The answer may matter more than many people realize.

Imagine two children entering first grade:

Jacob is bright, curious, and energetic. He struggles to sit still, frequently forgets to turn in his homework, and has difficulty following multi-step instructions. His teacher notices the symptoms and relays these to his parents. Jacob’s pediatrician conducts a comprehensive evaluation and diagnoses Jacob with ADHD.

His parents learn strategies to support him at home. His school implements accommodations. Later, medication becomes part of his treatment plan. Jacob still faces challenges, but he learns how his brain works and develops tools to succeed. He graduates from high school with strong grades and heads off to college.

[Webinar Replay: “6 Principles for Raising a Child with ADHD”]

Now imagine Maya. She is also intelligent, but her suspected ADHD symptoms present differently. She daydreams in class and quietly falls behind. She is emotionally sensitive, often taking criticism to heart and becoming overwhelmed by setbacks that her peers seem to brush off. Her teachers describe her as capable but inconsistent. Because she isn’t disruptive, her struggles are attributed to a lack of effort, disorganization, anxiety, or immaturity.

Year after year, she works harder while feeling increasingly frustrated. As academic demands increase, so do her feelings of self-doubt and inadequacy. By the time she receives an ADHD diagnosis at age 16, her grades have suffered, her confidence has eroded, and she is considering leaving school altogether.

The paths of these two children, one who received early intervention with medication and one who remained untreated until her mid-teens, illustrate a growing concern in ADHD care: It’s not simply about whether children are diagnosed, but when.

[Self-Test: Could Your Preschooler Have ADHD?]

A recent study published in JAMA Psychiatry examined educational outcomes among more than 580,000 individuals with ADHD. The researchers found that children diagnosed earlier in life had significantly better educational outcomes than those diagnosed during adolescence. Earlier diagnosis with associated with higher grade point averages, a greater likelihood of pursuing academic educational tracks, and substantially lower rates of school dropout.

The differences were striking. Individuals diagnosed during the early elementary school years had school dropout rates of approximately 9 percent by age 20, compared with nearly 30 percent among those first diagnosed around age 16. Similar patterns were observed in both males and females. The researchers concluded that adolescents diagnosed later represent a particularly vulnerable group.

The findings do not suggest that a diagnosis alone changes a child’s future. Rather, earlier recognition creates opportunities for intervention. Children identified sooner can access school accommodations, behavioral supports, parent education, and, when appropriate, medication – before years of academic struggles, self-doubt, and declining confidence take hold and begin to shape their identity.

This raises an important question: How many Mayas are sitting in classrooms today, struggling silently while waiting years for recognition or a diagnosis?

Universal ADHD screening offers a potential solution. Many children with ADHD are identified only after academic, social, or emotional difficulties have become impossible to ignore. Screening creates an opportunity to recognize concerns earlier before the consequences of undiagnosed ADHD begin to accumulate. Importantly, screening is not diagnosis. It is simply an invitation to look more closely.

Many critics of universal screening worry about overdiagnosis or unnecessary labeling. Those concerns deserve consideration. However, screening itself does not determine whether a child has ADHD. It merely identifies children who may benefit from a more comprehensive evaluation, meaning that fewer children with significant challenges get overlooked.

Growing evidence suggests that every year of delayed recognition carries educational and emotional costs that are difficult to reverse. Universal screening will not identify every child. But it may help more children find the right path before they wander too far afield.

For many children, the greatest risk may not be receiving an ADHD diagnosis. It may be never receiving one at all.

Here are three actions that caregivers can take:

Greg Mattingly, M.D., is an associate clinical professor at Washington University School of Medicine and co-chair of U.S. Psych Congress.

Thursday Night Football: Jaguars blow out Ravens 33-10

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Jaguars didn’t eat the cheese. They didn’t overlook the Ravens.

Jacksonville cruised to a 33-10 victory, moving to 6-2. Baltimore lost its fourth consecutive game to fall to 4-5.

Without starting quarterback Lamar Jackson and backup Tyler Huntley, and with undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano making his first start, the Ravens’ game plan was simple: Run the ball and shorten the game. They didn’t stand a chance despite 34 runs and 19 passes and a slight edge in time of possession (30:45 to 29:15).

The Jaguars won for the eighth time by 20-plus points since the start of last season.

The Ravens didn’t have a first down in the second half until Justice Hill ran for a 65-yard touhdown with 7:15 remaining. Baltimore ended with three first downs in the second half and 10 for the game.

The Jaguars outgained the Ravens 319 to 227, and the visitors forced Baltimore into three turnovers. Jourdan Lewis and Montaric Brown each had an interception of Fagnano, and linebacker Jalen McLeod forced and recovered a fumble by LaJohntay Wester on a kickoff.

Fagnano finished 10-of-18 for 60 yards and two interceptions. Hill had nine carries for 85 yards, and Henry moved up to seventh on the NFL’s all-time rushing list with 57 yards on 17 carries.

Lawrence went 16-of-26 for 242 yards and a touchdown. Jakobi Meyers had four catches for 65 yards; rookie tight end Nate Boerkircher caught two passes for 61 yards; and Parker Washington had two catches for 61 yards and a touchdown.

Bhayshul Tuten had 12 carries for 29 yards and a touchdown, and Ameer Abdullah had seven carries for 30 yards.

The Hidden Benefit Of Being Retired In A Bear Market

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As my public equity portfolio gets obliterated thanks to being overweight tech stocks, something interesting happened. Once I stopped feeling sorry for myself, I started to look on the bright side.

We're not in an official bear market, yet. But plenty of individual tech names have already fallen by over 20%, and when you're overweight those names, the index's official status doesn't matter much.

So I thought it might be helpful for those who are retired, or who are close, to discuss the positives of going through a bear market. We'll also review how long corrections usually last so we can better prepare.

As the midterm elections showed, a lot of people are not happy with the way our country has been led. But the discontent didn't appear overnight. The stock market was simply acting as a giant dose of Novocain.

As long as stocks kept going up, we could more easily bear higher oil prices, higher food prices, higher housing costs, higher college tuition costs, and explosive medical costs. After all, our investment returns were growing faster than inflation. A $9 dozen of eggs stings a lot less when your brokerage account is up $50,000 for the year.

But that tolerance for rising costs disappears the moment our investments start losing money. Suddenly, the eggs feel personal.

When the wealth effect vanishes, consumers cut back. Fewer dinners out. Fewer vacations. The new car gets pushed out another year. With declining corporate profitability, companies accelerate layoffs to protect margins. The newly unemployed tighten spending even further.

Rinse and repeat until economists officially declare a recession six months after everybody already felt it.

If you see the writing on the wall, you want want to get ahead of the layoffs by negotiating your own severance package. If you do, you will probably have an easier time getting a much better exit offer than being lumped with the masses.

If you're newly retired, your biggest fear is sequence of returns risk. That's the risk of suffering big losses early in retirement while you're also withdrawing money to live on. Do that for too long and your portfolio may never recover.

Lose enough and you get forced back to work. Nobody who has tasted freedom wants to return to Monday morning status meetings and performance reviews from someone half their age.

Retirees who live off their investments should feel a bear market more than the average worker. We don’t have a paycheck to cushion the blow. We may not have much spare cash flow to buy the dip either.

But here's the key point. If your investments can survive the correction or bear market, you should actually feel good during a downturn. Not ecstatic, but relatively good.

How do you know if you can survive? Run through this quick checklist:

So how long will the pain last? History offers some comfort, provided you have the patience of a parent waiting for a toddler to put on his shoes.

According to Capital Group, the S&P 500 has dropped 10% or more about once every 18 months since 1954. A 20%+ bear market shows up about once every six years.

In other words, if you plan on a 40-year retirement, expect roughly 25 corrections and six or seven bear markets. Declines are the price of admission, not a sign the world is ending. Get used to them.

Source: Capital Group, S&P 500 data from 1954 through 2025.

Hartford Funds, using Ned Davis Research data going back to 1928, counts 27 bear markets. Stocks fell 35% on average and took 289 days, or about 9.6 months, to hit bottom.

Bear markets were far more common during the Depression era. Since 1945, there have been 15, or one about every 5.1 years.

Two caveats before you get too comfortable. First, these figures measure peak to trough. Getting back to your old high takes longer. Second, the 1973 and 1980 bear markets each dragged on for more than 600 days. Averages are nice until you're living through the outlier.

But here's the part that should make retirees feel better. Every S&P 500 correction since 1929 has been followed by a recovery, and the average return in the first year after the bottom was 52%.

Hartford also found that about 42% of the S&P 500's strongest days over the past 20 years occurred during bear markets. Sell in a panic and you'll likely miss the rebound.

My takeaway: plan on gutting out roughly 12 to 18 months of pain from peak to trough, with a longer tail to fully recover. That's why the one to two years of cash is fine, especially with interest rates still high.

The existential dilemma of a bear market is this:

The correct answer is to work less hard and YOLO more. Let me explain using a concept I call Return On Effort (ROE).

During a bull market, working less hard actually increases your Return On Effort because your investments provide most of the returns. Unless you have a sales job or some other role tightly correlated to performance, an extra 15 hours a week rarely moves the needle. You're better off riding the organization's growth.

During a bear market, working hard to run in place or lose money makes even less sense. There's nothing you can do to stem declining consumer confidence, new socialist policies, higher taxes, and so forth. Your boss isn't handing out raises while revenue is falling. See Google's collapse after having such a huge lead in the AI race.

If anything, your extra effort earns you the privilege of doing the work of three colleagues who just got laid off. Congratulations on your 0% raise.

So you work less hard to protect your declining Return On Effort. Not because you're lazy. Because you're rational.

With this logic in mind, who works the least? The unemployed, the traditional retiree, and the early retiree.

Therefore, so long as your investments can withstand the beating, you're going to be just fine.

Happiness is relative. During a bull market, plenty of early retirees feel a twinge of FOMO watching former colleagues get promoted and cash fat RSU checks. During a bear market, that FOMO flips. Nobody envies the guy grinding through a reorg while his stock comp gets cut in half.

Whether you’re retired or still grinding away, a downturn offers some real perks.

1) Things get cheaper. When demand falls, so do prices for travel, hotels, and eventually real estate. Retirees with flexible schedules can travel off-peak and score deals that working parents can only dream about. Workers looking to buy their first home may finally face less competition.

2) You get to shop the sale. Retirees with passive income and a cash buffer get to buy when others are forced to sell. Being a liquidity provider during a panic is how fortunes are made. Workers get an even steadier advantage. Every paycheck and 401(k) contribution now picks up more shares at lower prices. Steady income plus a long time horizon makes you the natural buyer in a bear market.

3) Your kids get to buy the dip. I’ve written about how to use stock market downturns to make your kids millionaires. A child has 50+ years of compounding ahead. A 30% drop today is a rounding error by the time they’re in their 50s.

4) Tax moves get better. Tax-loss harvesting offsets future gains. Roth conversions at depressed valuations mean you pay less tax on the same number of shares, and all the recovery happens tax-free.

5) The AI hedge is doing its job. With many AI stocks down a lot, my investments stink. But there’s a flip side. If AI companies are struggling to justify their valuations, AI probably isn’t wiping out white-collar work as fast as everyone feared. That’s comforting for our kids, and for every worker wondering if their job will exist in 10 years.

6) You get humbled and build resilience. Being heavily overweight tech felt brilliant for years. Bear markets remind us we’re not as smart as we thought. Every downturn you survive makes you a calmer, better investor for the next one. If you’re still working, just make sure your emergency fund is solid, because layoffs tend to show up at the worst time.

You can't control tech volatility, interest rates, or who wins elections. But you can control your time.

The market will eventually recover. It always has. When it does, the people who grinded through the downturn will be exhausted. Those of who leaned into family, friends, and fitness will be well rested and ready to deploy capital.

Go play in the park. Take your kids to the beach. A bear market is the best time to enjoy the freedom you worked so hard to build.

Related: Why It's Better To Retire In A Bear Market Than A Bull Market

Readers, are you finding it more comforting to be retired during a downturn, or more stressful? How much of your portfolio would need to fall before you'd seriously consider going back to work? Do you agree that working less hard in a bear market is the rational move, or is that just a cop-out for the lazy? And for those still working, has the market selloff made you rethink how hard you're grinding? Are you using the downturn to fund your kids' investment accounts?

If you want a roadmap for getting through downturns and coming out richer, pick up a copy of Millionaire Milestones: Simple Steps To Seven Figures, a USA Today bestseller. It covers how to keep investing when everybody else is scared, so you can reach financial independence sooner.

I’ve been writing from firsthand experience since 2009, and I’ll keep sharing what I’m seeing and doing with my own money during this downturn.

45 Years Later, Indiana Jones' Best Line Is Still The Greatest Quote In Adventure Movie History

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Since Harrison Ford first brought Indiana Jones to life 45 years ago, one of his best still hasn't been topped in the action-adventure genre. Doctor Henry Jones Jr. has remained the gold standard for adventure protagonists (that's what happens when you have a character born from the minds of Steven Spielberg and George Lucas). Likewise, one line from Jones in particular embodies the core essence of the entire franchise at large.

Across its five movies, several great characters from the Indiana Jones franchise have delivered plenty of unforgettable dialogue and truly great lines. This includes Indy's dread while voicing his enduring fear of snakes, Short Round telling people to "hold onto their potatoes", or the surviving Grail Knight's warning to "choose wisely." However, one simple line from Raiders of the Lost Ark arguably captures everything that makes Indiana Jones so great as the peak action-adventure hero.

Despite being over four decades since the franchise first began, this one quote remains one of the greatest lines in adventure movie history overall.

Said in 1981's Raiders of the Lost Ark, after René Belloq and the Nazis claim the Ark of the Covenant for themselves, Indiana tells Sallah that he intends to follow the Nazis and their convoy. When Sallah asks how, Indy gives the most epically honest response: "I don't know, I'm making this up as I go." We then see Indy grabbing a horse and pursuing the Nazis in one of the franchise's very best chase sequences, ultimately securing the Ark by the scene's end and arranging to have the powerful artifact transported to London.

As great as the Raiders action scene is, the preceding line about making things up perfectly proves Indy often has no choice but to improvise more than he has time to create a full plan. As such, the quote is easily one of Jones' best, representing what makes him so great as a hero to begin with. Although Jones is a highly knowledgeable and talented archaeologist with an impressive understanding of history, that doesn't mean he instantly knows what to do in any given situation.

Ultimately, Indiana Jones is as fallible and capable of making wrong decisions as anyone else, and the above quote does an incredible job showcasing both Indy's confidence as well as his uncertainty, proving he doesn't need to be invincible to be an effective adventure hero. Likewise, one of the best strenghts of the franchise is that Indy's adventures are never entirely predictable. He constantly has to adapt to all kinds of obstacles and new threats, whether he's going after the Ark of the Covenant, Sankara Stones, The Holy Grail, Crystal Skulls, or even the Dial of Destiny.

The "making it up" quote is also a great example of how the Indiana Jones franchise has maintained a solid balance of thrilling action and comedy. Indy might be facing an incredibly dangerous situation, but there's almost always room for some sarcastic remarks or one-liners, ensuring that the movies stay light and engaging throughout (and not too serious).

All things considered, it's pretty easy to see why this particular quote from Indiana Jones himself has aged so well. Rather than giving some sort of grand declaration of determined heroism to stop the Nazis with a well-thought-out battle plan, Indy says he'll figure it out before grabbing a horse and doing exactly that. That relatability and desire to do the right thing despite not fully knowing exactly how to make it happen or what comes next is a huge reason why Harrison Ford's Indiana Jones has remained one of the very best adventure heroes of all time over four decades after his initial debut.

Created by George Lucas, Indiana Jones is an action-adventure franchise that began with 1981's Raiders of the Lost Ark. The series follows Dr. Henry "Indiana" Jones, Jr., an archaeologist who adventures across the world as he faces off against the likes of Nazis and other groups bent on collecting ancient artifacts of power. Seeking to preserve history, Jones races against his incidental foes to stop them from conquering - or destroying- the world.

Taiwan Creative Content Fest Opens as Chair Sue Wang Says ‘Culture Is Power’ Amid AI Shift

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sue Wang, chair of the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), opened the seventh Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) in Taipei on Friday by saying artificial intelligence is reshaping how the creative industries, and even human society, are defined.

“We are all asking: Does cultural content still have a future?” Wang said.

She said AI could take on technical tasks but could not replace the elements of creative work that move people. Wang urged Taiwan to put its technology strengths to work in ways that serve people and improve their lives. “I hope more people believe that culture is power. Especially in this rapidly changing era, cultural content can help create a happier society,” she said. Related Stories Japan IP Market to Return to Cannes in 2027 as Tokyo Partnership Continues Neon Partners With 'NAZA' Filmmakers, Guardian and MK2 to Bring Prize-Winning Doc to Wider Audiences

Wang also called this year’s edition an important milestone for the industry. “Everyone is working hard to build partnerships and integration, both horizontally and vertically. Cross-sector, cross-platform and cross-border collaboration has become a trend,” she said.

The event features 107 Market exhibitors from seven countries and 92 project and story pitches spanning film, series, animation, documentaries, publishing and comics. Industry participants from 28 countries are attending. The four-day Forum will hold 12 sessions with more than 20 speakers, addressing international co-production, streaming-platform consolidation, IP, short-form content and AI.

Hosted by Taiwanese actor Allison Lin, the opening ceremony was attended by Culture Minister Li Yuan; Wang Ting-an, executive secretary of the National Development Fund under the Executive Yuan; Weta FX CEO Daniel Seah; Toei Company President and CEO Yoshimura Fumio; Frédéric Laplanche, director of the French Office in Taipei; Fernando Mariano Schmidt Hernández, director of the Chilean Trade Office in Taipei; and Marco Lombardi of the Italian Economic, Trade and Cultural Promotion Office in Taipei, among many other local and international luminaries.

Li, who chaired the evaluation committee at TCCF’s first edition, said he had watched the event grow from an exhibition into a market and an international platform. “Now we’re thinking about the future of Taiwan’s cultural content ecosystem and how TCCF can become an Asian creative-content brand, helping cultural workers across Asia and around the world find opportunities and funding. That is one of the key goals behind the Ministry of Culture and TAICCA,” he said.

TCCF runs Nov. 6-9 at Taipei Nangang Exhibition Center, Hall 2.

Te Araroa day 11: Failing to find a cafe

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

19.5km, the Stone Store (Kerikeri) to Waitangi Holiday Park

We got moving a bit earlier than the day before, as our kind host Amelia was going to drop us at the start of our walk on her way to work. The three of us squashed our packs into the boot of her little Mazda, and I tried valiently to shut the door. After a number of attempts, slamming it progressively harder each time, the ‘open door’ light went off and we were away.

We were back at the Stone Store where we had left off at around 8.30am. Zoe was maybe going to meet us at the start, so Ruby called her to find out where she was at. As it turned out, she could see us from the top of the hill we were about to walk up.

The track went a little bit further along the river and up the backs of people’s houses before popping out at the road. There was a pavement for the first bit, which suddenly ended in classic NZ fashion. But this did provide a photo opportunity, as there was a ‘Te Araroa trail users’ warning sign that I wanted a picture with. There were some earlier, but I had been alone at the time. In all honesty, this was not a very good spot for a photo op. But no hikers were harmed and we proceeded down the road for another kilometre or so.

Soon after, we turned into a pine forest. The trail went along some gravel roads, which were not the most exciting walking but were at least better than the main road. Despite warnings about logging trucks, we saw only two cars.

The view for the day: gravel road and pine forest

As we walked, Ruby mentioned that there was a nice cafe at the Waitangi treaty grounds, which we would be passing later in the day. There was also a little wooden sign for a cafe which was supposed to be open Friday to Monday. This day was Monday but it was difficult to believe that there would be a cafe along these roads! Eventually we stopped for lunch at a bench. Part of the route went through a mountain bike park, which did have a cafe at the entrance. But alas, it was closed. We were about to continue on when Lucy appeared from the forest. She joined the rest of us as we got out to the paved road.

Most people were planning to walk to the Paihia Top 10 holiday park. But Lucy wanted to go to the Waitangi treaty grounds, and I had an exciting side-quest for the next day! So we were both going to stay at the Waitangi holiday park instead. Eventually we got to the treaty grounds, and attempted to find the cafe. But it was closed! We weren’t having much luck with cafes today.

Approaching the Waitangi Treaty Grounds, via the golf club; ‘Where the tee meets the sea’

For anyone interested: The Treaty of Waitangi is considered the founding document of New Zealand, although it isn’t without its controversies. It was signed in 1840 in Waitangi, near Paihia, and the grounds today are a historical site you can visit. I think there is a museum and such, although I haven’t been inside myself.

Soon we reached the turning for the holiday park Lucy and I were going to stay at. It was only 1.45pm and I hoped we weren’t too early to check in! I was assigned a site at the far end of the campground, and found the neighbouring site occupied by a tent so small it could only belong to a thru-hiker. Indeed, the occupant was another British woman called Georgia, who is doing TA. Something I’ve been noticing is that there are a lot more solo women than I expected. I thought we would be a bit of a rare breed, but in fact the majority of other hikers I’ve met so far have been solo women! At Blackbridge campsite the other night, there were about 12 people and only two were men. There was one couple, and the other 9 were women hiking alone. Which is pretty cool!

I got set up and decided to go to the nearby supermarket to get dinner for that night and the next. I figured that I might as well splash out on some heavy stuff, since I wouldn’t need to carry it far. In the Woolworths I ended up running into Annabelle and Mauritz. Their group had struggled to find accomodation in Kerikeri so ended up taking a rest day in Paihia instead.

I met a friendly cat on the way back from the supermarket

Back at the campsite, I was feeling a bit flat. I had got so used to having people around me for the last few days that it felt odd to be alone. Combined with the short day, I felt a bit purposeless. But I knew I wanted to get some blogs done, so I got stuck in and the time went by reasonably quickly.

After dinner, I went back to my tent and found that the picnic table near my site had been colonised by some British boys and an American from either side of me. I had spoken to the American earlier, as his pack, tent, and the fact he arrived on foot had me thinking he was a hiker. But it seemed he was just travelling around, and sounded very impressed when I told him about TA. Sometimes I get so engrossed in the world of thru-hiking that I forget that it is actually a pretty cool and impressive thing to do. It’s nice to be reminded of that sometimes!

I hoped the three of them wouldn’t be too loud or keep talking too late, as they were literally two metres from my tent. I wanted to do some more writing but I couldn’t help listening in to their conversation. It seemed that the British boys were on their gap year after going to boarding school with some of the royal family. The American was travelling around the world after earning a lot of money doing a lucrative job – I didn’t quite catch what, but I think it was something in service of the super-rich. It was funny to hear their opinions and observations as recently-arrived travellers to New Zealand. They all sounded very young – and I know, I’m not exactly old either! But when I think back to how I was when I just finished school, I can appreciate how much I’ve matured.

Anyway, they didn’t talk too late, and I was able to write more blogs and go to bed at a reasonable time.

Mormon Wives' Zac Affleck Admits to "Mistakes" in Jen Affleck Marriage

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star Zac Affleck shared what went wrong in his marriage to Jen Affleck that led her to file for divorce, admitting he "begged" her to stay.

Zac Affleck is still coming to terms with Jen Affleck's decision.

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star shared what led to the end of their seven-year marriage, admitting he "begged" Jen to stay when she first brought up divorce a year ago and was surprised to learn she officially submitted paperwork on Nov. 3.

"I found out from a TMZ reporter about the filing," Zac shared in a Nov. 5 TikTok. "But regardless of that, this is something we've been talking about for a long time. Jen came to me about a year ago, after Dancing With the Stars, and told me she was unhappy and wanted a divorce. And I basically begged her to stay, told her work on things, fix things, become whoever she needed me to be."

During that time, Jen became pregnant with their fourth child.

But now that the reality star—with whom he also shares kids Nora, 5, Luca, 3, and Penelope, 16 months—is moving forward with the divorce, Zac said he wanted to "shut down any narratives" about the split.

"I don't want anyone speaking bad about Jen," he continued. "I'll be the first to say I've made plenty of mistakes in our marriage, and it just really was time for for us to go our own ways. We got married so young, and it sucks because I really did feel like we had something pretty special when we got married."

But after tying the knot in Newport Beach, Calif., in 2019, the 30-year-old said "life happened" and they grew apart.

"We had four kids. We signed up for reality TV on a show that's really hurt a lot of people's lives," Zac noted. "Marriage is hard. Having your marriage in the public is hard. Raising kids is hard. And I think slowly over time we lost that spark."

While he said "these last couple years have taken a pretty massive toll," the reality star shared that he hopes he can now turn his attention toward new career goals, such as re-enrolling in medical school, starting a business or pursuing a PhD.

Most of all, Zac said he's committed to having a "healthy" coparenting relationship with Jen as she prepares to give birth.

"That's really my priorities," he shared. "I feel like probably the thing that's eating me alive the most is I feel like I failed my kids, and I'm worried that I'm going to cause them damage. And I just hope that we can still give them a normal childhood, but as far as that goes, Jen and I are definitely on the same page of wanting to give them the best the best life we can."

For a closer look at more couples from The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, keep reading.

Taylor Frankie Paul & Dakota Mortensen: Broken Up

While Taylor Frankie Paul's romance with Dakota Mortensen—they welcomed son Ever in 2024—bloomed on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, they called it quits due to Dakota's infidelity.

"Right after Ever was born, Dakota and I were living together and in bliss with our new baby, and I was even considering engagement," Taylor—who began dating the real estate agent in July 2022—revealed during the season two premiere of the Hulu series. "And then I learned that he was lying this entire time."

Taylor was told by a woman named Jenna that she and Dakota had been intimate and he had "spent the night more than once." (He has denied the claims, writing in a September 2024 TikTok that he "never cheated.")

On the series, Taylor said that Dakota accused Jenna of instigating their affair.

"No, it was him," she emphasized in the confessional. "He said he had just kissed her on the couch. No, he went on the couch, they hooked up on the couch, and then they went to the room and they hooked up."

Since ending her romance with the Basin Tiling founder, the mom of three (she shares two kids with ex-husband Tate Paul) was set to find love once again on season 22 of The Bachelorette, but things got a bit thorny when news broke in March 2026 of an alleged confrontation between the exes weeks earlier.

A spokesperson for the Draper City Police Department confirmed to People that there was an open "domestic assault investigation," telling the outlet "allegations have been made in both directions.”

Days later, a video of a previous 2023 physical altercation between Taylor and Dakota surfaced online, prompting ABC to cancel the MomToker's season of the dating franchise.

As for Dakota, he has kicked off a romance with show guest star Shinia Powell, though he previously apologized to Taylor for hooking up with her during a season four episode.

Whitney Leavitt & Connor Leavitt: Married

Whitney Leavitt found the perfect dance partner in Connor Leavitt, whom she wed in 2016. But it wasn't always smooth sailing for the couple—who share kids Sedona, Liam and Billy—as they navigated life in the spotlight.

For instance, the Dancing With the Stars alum, who announced during her Broadway run in May 2026 that she'd be leaving Mormon Wives, had to shut down rumors about her husband's sexuality.

"Do I need to put out a sex tape, or something? Like, trust me," she said on the Off the Vine podcast in October 2024. "We're good. Everything's fine."

"I think it's because when we were going through our personal stuff—and I know you haven't seen it—but he struggled through a porn addiction," she explained. "There's a scene where one of my friends says something along the lines of how they could see how he was confused sexually."

Through the ups and downs, the reality star emphasized that she and Connor—who were 23 and 22, respectively, when they got married—are stronger than ever. In fact, they even renewed their vows in February 2026 for their 10th wedding anniversary.

"We got married really young," Conner told Brides after their ceremony. "It felt like our wedding day, as much as it was for us, it really felt like it was for our families. So we really want to do something to make it ours, and this felt like a really fun way to do it."

Married in 2019, Jen Affleck and Zac Affleck—no relation to Ben Affleck, despite jokes to the contrary—have been at the center of breakup rumors since SLOMW's debut.

In fact, their fight after Jen attended a Chippendales striptease show in Las Vegas became a focal point of the first season. The couple—who share kids Nora, Penelope and Lucas—made up, however, and the Dancing With the Stars contestant later defended their relationship.

"It can be tiring and draining, just because it gets old having to hear the same things," Jen told E! News of the speculations in November 2025. "For me, I'm like, 'People will always talk and people will always have their opinions.' I think it is frustrating for him when people don't see what's happening behind closed doors and how much work and effort he's putting in."

But while Jen announced in June 2026 that she'd be adding to their dance troupe with baby No. 4, the MomToker filed for divorce that November after seven years of marriage.

Jessi Draper & Jordan Ngatikaura: Divorced

Bumps in Jessi Draper and Jordan Ngatikaura's five-year marriage emerged after the hairstylist admitted to having an emotional affair with Vanderpump Villa's Marciano Brunette, though initially it appeared they were working towards smoother roads.

"We are back together," Jessi—who shares kids Jagger and Jovi with Jordan—told E! News in November 2025. "We're trying to work on it. Relationships are not perfect. I think a situation like ours—it's going to take time. It's not going to something that's just amazing overnight."

Sadly, the reconciliation appeared to be short-lived, with Jordan also dad to daughter Peyton from a previous relationship, filing for divorce in March 2026 after five years of marriage.

However, days later, Jessi said she was “blindsided” by his filing and accused him of rushing to submit the paperwork because “he wanted the headline first.”

Admitting that her “heart sunk” seeing the news, she explained, “because we agreed to tell our children together before filing and we haven’t done that.” (E! reached out to attorneys for Jordan for comment but haven’t heard back.)

Though Jessi told E! News that she was "just casually dating and living life," when she brought Marciano as her date to the 2026 American Music Awards in May 2026, he revealed a tattoo dedicated to her that August.

Jessi and Jordan finalized their divorce in September 2026.

Mayci didn't expect to find love with Jacob Neeley. In fact, after the unexpected death of her boyfriend Arik Mack in 2015, when she was just 14 weeks pregnant with their son Hudson, she was prepared to be a single mom.

But it all changed when she met Jacob in 2016 at age 21, whom she would marry two years later.

"After everything I'd been through, I was very guarded, but Jacob was kind and we got on really well," Mayci said in her memoir Told You So. "For a long time, we remained just friends and because we weren't really dating, I was OK with him meeting my son."

"Jacob was amazing with him, which made him appear even more attractive," she continued. "I was terrified of getting hurt again but, after four months, I couldn't deny my feelings any longer. He was kind and funny and made me feel safe."

Mayci and Jacob went on to welcome daughters Harlow in 2021 and Charli in 2025.

Demi Engemann and her husband Bret Engemann tied the knot in 2021, one year after she and Blake Corbin divorced. As the couple's relationship played out on the reality show, their sexual explorations became a major plot point after Whitney gifted Demi a box of Fruity Pebbles.

The truth behind the joke gift wasn't revealed until Jessi shared on Instagram in November 2025 that "grandpa Bret likes to drink her piss like it's dirty soda and he gulps it down like water."

But Demi and Bret aren't crying over spilled secrets—or pee. In fact, she told Entertainment Tonight that December that she and Bret "have nothing else left to do other than own it and have fun with it."

Although their sex life raised eyebrows, the couple has been embracing life with their blended family. While Demi shares daughter Maude with her ex, Bret is dad to his and The Real Housewives of Salt Lake City alum Angie Harrington's sons Rome and Cole.

"I've never seen a dad so involved or love their kids harder, the most devoted step-dad to Maude, and future father to our own children one day," Demi wrote on Instagram in June 2024. "Your ability to remain consistently loyal to your role as father/step-father while navigating all that life throws your way, will never cease to amaze me!"

Mikayla Matthews and Jace Terry have been through it all together. While The reality star first met Jace in 2015 when she was 15 and they welcomed their first son Beckham two years later. Since then, the couple—who tied the knot in 2018—welcomed kids Haven, Tommie and Lottie.

While Mikayla and Jace have been loving life with their four kids, Mikayla admitted that she hopes to grow their family even further.

"When people ask me how many kids I want, I'm like, 'I've always wanted three or four, but I could see myself having six,'" she told People in February 2025. "I'm excited to hopefully move into a new house. I want a big lot for my herd of kids."

However, Mikayla confirmed in the ninth episode of the show's fourth season that she and Jace were taking time apart amid ongoing intimacy struggles.

As Mikayla began unpacking her trauma surrounding years of sexual abuse as a teen, as well as her chronic illness—which causes flare-ups due to heightened stress—their marriage had been deeply affected.

"I think it's hard anytime you bring up the stuff with the sex or the intimacy," she admitted to Jace. "I feel like it's hard on me 'cause I feel like, again, my nervous system's already in fight or flight and I'm already maxed out. My bucket is full."

After Layla Taylor and Clayton Wessel—who are parents to sons Oliver and Maxwell—divorced in 2023, the reality star teased her new romance with Mason McWhorter in July 2025.

Their relationship was a bit complicated on paper. Hang with us: Mason's brother is Chase McWhorter, the ex-husband of Layla's costar Miranda McWhorter. But the pair had been willing to put their romance to the test.

"Right now, I'm kind of scared of marriage but I just want to get to know somebody very well before I even consider that," Layla told Us in November 2025. "I'm just happy and content where me and Mason are. And if somehow that ever were to come up, then we would see."

Unfortunately, the mom of two confirmed that she and Mason had split in February 2026.

"I think we were just on two different paths right now, and he felt in order for him to grow, he had to be on his own," Layla shared. "I respect his decision, and I truly hope that he gets what he wants in life."

As for Layla, who came out as bisexual in June 2026, she soft-launched her romance with a mystery woman a month later in an Instagram video. She later seemed to confirm her relationship with Colorado-based content creator Kelsie Chesser.

Miranda McWhorter & Chase McWhorter: Divorced

Although Miranda is a newer addition to The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, joining in season two, she played a major role in the infamous MomTok "soft swinging scandal." Despite the situation with Taylor, Miranda revealed that drama wasn't the reason for her 2024 divorce from Chase.

"The reasons why we decided to split were things that were issues far before that,” she explained on the Weekly Trash podcast in August 2024. "That might not have helped, for sure, but it definitely did not play a part in the decision process."

Nonetheless, she and Chase have been committed to parenting their kids Brooks and Cohen.

"The thing with him and I," she continued, "we've always had a pretty good foundation of a friendship prior to even having a romantic relationship. I feel like that's something that set us up for some level of success when it comes to co-parenting."

Fox Officially Replaces ‘9-1-1’ in 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

When 9-1-1 hit the airwaves in 2018, it was a massive hit right away. With a cast boasting big names such as Angela Bassett and Peter Krause, and the pedigree of Ryan Murphy behind it, the first-responder drama instantly provided tons of fast-paced action and high-octane rescues. But by 2023, Fox was realizing that the series was costing them a pretty penny to produce. With enormous production costs of approximately $9 million an episode and a pricey licensing fee for Disney's 20th Television (which owns the rights to the show), Fox decided to throw in the towel with 9-1-1. The series moved on over to ABC, but two years later, Fox is still searching for a proper replacement for the first-responder procedural. The good news is that they just might have found it now.

In 2020, Fox released a spin-off of the original 9-1-1 series called 9-1-1: Lone Star, featuring Rob Lowe and Gina Torres at the helm and a brand-new location in Austin, Texas. However, as soon as the flagship series moved over to ABC in 2023, 9-1-1: Lone Star seemed to have lost some of its mojo (and definitely lost some of its viewers). The loss of synergy of the two shows airing on the same network definitely impacted the series, and its cancellation was announced in 2024, with its series finale airing in February 2025.

Four action series. One of them is built around exactly the way you handle pressure, people, and a room that has just gone very quiet. Here is where you would survive the trilogy, and probably headline it.

You are precision with a grudge. You don't posture, you don't escalate, and you don't warn anybody twice. That is exactly why the people who know you are so careful never to be the reason you start.

You understand the thing the whole franchise is secretly about: the cars are spectacular, but the crew is the point. You collect people, you keep them, and you'd drive through a building for any one of them.

You over-prepare and then take the dangerous part yourself anyway. It looks like control, but it's really responsibility. You have never worked out how to ask somebody else to carry the risk.

You are not the most trained person in the building, the best equipped, or the one anybody would have picked. You are simply the one still standing at the end, which turns out to be the only qualification that matters.

Fox did its best to find another first-responder procedural to air, this time locating the action in Hawaii. Rescue HI-Surf premiered in fall 2024, but was decried by critics and viewers for having way too much melodrama. Audiences just seemed to have a hard time connecting with the characters and storylines. This led to the series being cancelled after one season, leaving Fox in need of another first-responder procedural to fill the void.

The firefighters can certainly take the heat!

There have been rumblings for years now that a Baywatch reboot was being floated around Hollywood. The original series was a huge success, when it aired from 1989 to 2000. The 11-season run of the show became a pop culture obsession with those famous red bathing suits and sexy running-on-the-beach scenes. The series also demonstrated that first-responder procedurals could take place in unexpected locations with heroes that didn't look like traditional police officers or firefighters. It also helped further gender equality on television.

While some decried the women as being overly sexualized, the series also featured the female characters saving the day just as frequently as the men did. The drama also helped create mega-stars out of David Hasselhoff, Pamela Anderson, Yasmine Bleeth, and a young Jason Momoa. With the buzz the series created when it was on the air, it's not at all surprising that Fox would be interested in recreating some of that magic with its current lineup.

Fox announced that they'd be greenlighting a Baywatch revival in a straight-to-series order of 12 episodes. Several of the original creators will be returning to the project, and the showrunner will be Matt Nix, who created Burn Notice. The series is slated to premiere in January 2027, and there's already some exciting chatter about what we have in store for us. The series stars Arrow lead Stephen Amell, who plays Hobie Buchannon, the son of Mitch Buchannon (David Hasselhoff) and is a Baywatch Captain. Jessica Belkin plays Charlie Vale, Hobie's biological daughter who has just arrived in town from Texas. Hassie Harrison plays Nat, Hobie's best friend. The series also stars Noah Beck and Shay Mitchell. Original series stars David Chokachi and Erika Eleniak are back to reprise their roles, though Pamela Anderson has already confirmed she will not be joining the series. As it stands, the Baywatch procedural could be Fox's best chance at finally replacing 9-1-1.

The series already has an IP that people will recognize, which could be a reason why Rescue HI-Surf failed to take off. Most first-responder shows come with a built-in audience of some kind, and if the revival is working with some of the same team that made the original a success, Baywatch has a great chance at also making this new iteration a hit too.

Fox has been searching for the right series to finally boost their ratings back to where they were during the 9-1-1 days. Baywatch is a perfect replacement because many people will tune into the premiere just to see how close it is to the original. Any fans of the show from back in the day will likely help Fox get impressive numbers to start out with. As long as the new show brings the drama and excitement that the original was known for, the Baywatch reboot will likely stick around for some time to come.

The original Baywatch is available to watch on Tubi in the U.S.
